Let me think about this problem carefully.

We have System F with locally nameless representation, non-deterministic choice, and step relation `-->` which is call-by-value. We need to prove: for any closed `t : forall X. X -> X`, and closed well-formed type U, closed value v : U, `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`.

This is essentially the free theorem / parametricity result. We need a logical relation.

The standard approach: define a logical relation on terms indexed by type, using a "step-indexed" or "reduction to" style relation (since the language is non-deterministic and the conclusion only asserts existence of a reduction path).

Key challenge: with non-determinism, the relation needs to be "may" semantics: `t ~ T` means there exists a reduction path from t to some value v in the interpretation of T (for closed types), etc.

Let me think about what logical relation works. The target theorem says: t : forall X. X->X, U wf, v : U value, then (t U) v -->* v.

The classic parametricity result would be that `t [U]` behaves as identity on values of U. So the logical relation for `forall X. X -> X` at type U applied to v should give v.

Design a logical relation `R T v` for closed values v at closed type T, plus a term relation `E T t` meaning "t may reduce to some value in R T". Actually let's think of a relation defined on values indexed by type, where type variables are interpreted by arbitrary binary relations? Hmm, but for the target we just instantiate at U and want identity.

Standard approach for "theorems for free" via logical relations: interpret types as relations on terms (or on values). For the type `forall X. X -> X`, the relational interpretation at relation R (on values) is: the set of values `Lambda t` such that for all related (v, w) in R, application `t [X] v` reduces to something related to `t [X] w`... Actually the free theorem: for f : forall X. X->X and any relation R between U and V, if v R w then (f U v) R (f V w).

For the specific target with a single type, we want: for the identity relation on U's values (or some relation that relates v to itself), we get f U v -->* v.

But here we instantiate at U both sides: choose the relation R = {(v, v) : v is a value of U with v : U}. Hmm, but this relation must be "admissible" in the sense required for the logical relation on `forall`.

Let's think about what relation on terms is needed for the free theorem to be provable.

The standard "parametricity" proof for `forall X. X->X` applied to closed v: we can define, for a closed type U and value v: consider the relation R_v on values of type... hmm. Actually an easier approach: define the identity relation on all values of type U? Then f U v -->* v' where v' is related to v, i.e., v' = ... Hmm.

Wait, actually there's a known simpler approach: the logical relation for the "free theorem" can be established by taking relation R = { (x, x) : x is a value of type U }? That's the identity relation restricted to U-typed values. But then we need to show for the identity function... Actually, if the relation is the identity on values, then the free theorem gives (f U v) -->* v' with v' R v, i.e., v' = v. That works if the relation is admissible and the logical relation interpretation of `forall X. X->X` at R implies the desired conclusion.

But wait — careful. For the "may" semantics with non-determinism, "related" isn't just a value relation; we need a term relation that handles nondeterministic reduction. Let's design:

Define `R : ty -> value-relation` where for closed types with no free variables. Hmm, but the type interpretation is usually defined at all types with free variables mapped by an environment of relations. That's heavy but doable.

Simplification: Since the target theorem only needs one particular instantiation, maybe we can define the relation by recursion on the type with a "self-relation" only, avoiding general relational environments, as follows.

Standard trick: For proving free theorems of this form, use the logical relation with "identity" relation for type variables: define `V T v` (value v is in the interpretation of closed type T) where type variables are interpreted... but T has to be closed. Actually the type `Ty_All T` is handled: `V (Ty_All T) (tm_tabs t)` iff for all closed U and values v, ... hmm, but for the identity theorem we need the relation between different instantiations.

Let me think about the classic "free theorem" logical relation for `forall X. X -> X` at the two instantiations U and U (same). We need a binary relation between values of type U and values of type U. Actually we need a binary relation R between (values of the "left" type) and (values of the "right" type). If both types are U, we can pick R = identity on U-values? Then the free theorem says: for f in the interpretation of forall X. X->X (with the identity relation for type variables), for all values v of U, f[U] v reduces to a value related to ... hmm, but the interpretation of `forall X. X->X` with the identity relation as type variable interpretation is exactly the set of values `Lambda t` such that for all U and v, `t[U] v` may reduce to a value v' with v' R_U v, where R_U is identity on U-values... but only for the *same* U as in the type interpretation. Wait, if the type variable interpretation is a function from types U to relations on U-values, then the "forall" interpretation quantifies over all types U and uses R_U. With R_U = identity on U-values, we get (f U v) -->* v' where v' = v. 

So: define a unary logical relation on values `V T v` where T is a closed well-formed type, intended as "v is a value of type T (and in the semantic interpretation)"; but with the twist for type variables: since T must be closed, type variables can't appear free... except inside the `forall` interpretation? Let's see: `V (Ty_All T) (tm_tabs s)` should be defined as: for all closed U, v with... hmm, we want the relational interpretation. Let's do binary with "left type = right type = same closed type U but different values".

Hmm, but if we make the relation unary with "identity relation for type variables", then at `Ty_All T` we would need to interpret `open_ty T U` for arbitrary closed U, and the free type variable X becomes U — but U's values are related by identity too. That's exactly the same as the relational interpretation where R_X = identity on U's values. Indeed, substituting X := U and using relation "identity on U-values" gives the same result.

So we can define a unary relation `V T v` for closed T? Wait type variables can't appear free in a closed T. But inside `Ty_All T`, we open T with a closed U, so all types we interpret are closed. Good. So define:

- `V : ty -> tm -> Prop` ("v is in the value interpretation of closed type T").

But wait, "closed" is not enough — we need well-formedness w.r.t. the type context. Hmm, actually for the fundamental theorem we'll have `Delta` and types with free variables in Delta. The standard approach: define the relation relative to a type substitution `sigma : atom -> ty` mapping free variables to closed types, and interpret type variables by identity on ... hmm, but identity on what? The type substitution maps X to a closed type U_X; the relation at X would be identity on values of U_X.

Hmm, but for the free theorem, using identity relations for free variables is fine as long as we instantiate all free variables appropriately. So plan: binary relations are unnecessary; a unary "parametric" relation with the identity relation for type variables suffices for this particular theorem.
```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> ty) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
```

The relational interpretation of types (Reynolds): For types with free type variables from Delta, an environment assigns to each X ∈ Delta a type σ(X) (for the left) and τ(X) (for the right) plus a relation R_X ⊆ Val(σX) × Val(τX). Then:

- ⟦X⟧ = R_X
- ⟦T1 → T2⟧ = { (f, g) : ∀ a b, ⟦T1⟧(a,b) → ∃ a' b', f a ⇓ a', g b ⇓ b', ⟦T2⟧(a',b') }
- ⟦∀X. T⟧ = { (f, g) : ∀ U V (R ⊆ Val U × Val V), ∃ a b, f [U] ⇓ a, g [V] ⇓ b, ⟦T⟧_{R}(a,b) }  — where the body T is opened with... hmm, in locally nameless, T contains BVar 0; the environment for the bound variable should be R. If we open T with a fresh atom X, then the environment maps X to R, sigma maps X to U on the left and τ maps X to V on the right.

So the environment is really a triple per variable: (left type, right type, relation). In the diagonal case (left type = right type, relation = equality), we have σ = τ and R = equality for all variables. Then:
- ⟦X⟧ = equality.
- ⟦T1 → T2⟧ as above (with equality at leaves).
- ⟦∀X. T⟧ = { (f,f) : ∀ U (closed wf), ∃ a b, f[U] ⇓ a, f[U] ⇓ b, ⟦T⟧_{X ↦ (U,U,equality)}(a,b) }.

Now the key point: ⟦T⟧_{X ↦ (U,U,eq)} where T is open (contains X free) — interpreted by recursion on T with X ↦ equality. Then at T_TApp in the fundamental theorem: the term is `tapp t U` typed at `open_ty T_body U`. So the type in the judgment is `open_ty T_body U`, which is a type *without* the atom X but with U substituted. Let me look at this from a different angle: how do "theorems for free" proofs in Coq handle the type application case? I recall the "parametricity" proofs using a logical relation indexed by two interpretations of the type, with the key trick being that *type variables are interpreted by arbitrary relations* and *type substitution is handled by the fundamental theorem for the relational interpretation of types*, which includes a "Kripke-style" or "environment" approach.

The standard setup (from e.g. "Parametricity in an Impredicative Sort" or the "Reynolds' abstraction theorem"): Define for types T with free variables in Delta:
⟦T⟧_{ρ} where ρ maps each X ∈ Delta to a triple (A_X, B_X, R_X) with R_X ⊆ A_X × B_X. Then ⟦T⟧ ⊆ (values of type [ρ]T on the left) × (values of type [ρ']T on the right). The abstraction theorem: if Δ ⊢ t : T then for all ρ, [ρ]t ⟦T⟧_ρ [ρ']t. For T_TAbs: we need to show [ρ](tabs t) ⟦∀X.T'⟧_ρ [ρ'](tabs t), i.e., for all (A,B,R), the instantiated terms are related. We use the IH at environment ρ[X ↦ (A,B,R)]: it gives [ρ[X↦(A,B,R)]](open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) ⟦T'⟧_{ρ[X↦(A,B,R)]} [ρ'[...]](...). Now, [ρ[X↦A]](open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) = [ρ](t)[A] (type application), so this is exactly the needed instantiation. Good.

For T_TApp: t : ∀X.T', U : type. The judgment type is open_ty T' U. IH: [ρ]t ⟦∀X.T'⟧_ρ [ρ']t. To conclude [ρ](t[U]) ⟦open_ty T' U⟧_ρ [ρ'](t[U]), we need to instantiate the All interpretation at (A, B, R) := ([ρ]U, [ρ']U, ???). We know [ρ](t[U]) = [ρ]t[ρU] and [ρ'](t[U]) = [ρ']t[ρ'U]. Let me explore this because it might be much shorter than a full parametricity framework.

Key insight: terms of "type variable type" X in a context where X is a free type variable and the term context has a variable x:X. Claim: if has_type [X] (x:X) body X, then for any closed type U and closed value v : U [via substitution], body[X:=U][x:=v] -->* v. Hmm, but actually more generally we'd like: body reduces to (a value which is) a variable y with Gamma(y)=X? But Gamma = [x:X] only has x. Under T_Abs the Gamma might have more entries? For our target's derivation, after T_Abs, Gamma = [x:X] exactly. But the derivation of body : X might use T_TAbs which extends Delta, not Gamma. So Gamma stays [x:X]? T_TAbs extends Delta with a fresh Y and the same Gamma. So yes, within the derivation of body, Gamma is always [x:X] extended by more T_Abs... but wait, T_Abs extends Gamma. Can the derivation of body : X contain a T_Abs somewhere? The result type is X (a type variable), and T_Abs's conclusion type is an arrow. T_App's result type is T2 which could be X, and its premise has a function of type T1→X. That subderivation could contain a T_Abs producing an arrow type ending in X. E.g., body = app g w where g : Y→X, w : Y. Hmm, but where would g come from? Gamma = [x:X], so g can't be a variable of type Y→X. g could be an abstraction λy:Y. x' — no wait, with Gamma = [x:X], the only variable is x. But g could be a λ whose body is x : X. Then app (λy:Y. x) w -->* x. Fine.

So the syntactic claim: any body with has_type Delta Gamma X where X is a free type variable in Delta... and Gamma containing a variable of type X... reduces to a variable of type X. But the choice of which variable is determined by the term. In our target, after T_Abs, Gamma = [x:X] exactly. But the derivation might use T_Var with a different variable if there were shadowing—no, only x.

Hmm, wait. Careful: in the target, t : ∀X. X→X with Delta=[] Gamma=empty. The derivation must end with T_TAbs (since the type is ∀X...). Then the premise: has_type [X] empty (open_tm_ty t0 (FVar X)) (open_ty T0 (FVar X)) where T0 = Arrow (BVar 0) (BVar 0), so open_ty T0 (FVar X) = Arrow X X. Then this derivation must end with... it could be T_TAbs again, T_Choice, T_TApp, T_Abs, T_App, T_Var. Since the type is an arrow, T_Abs or T_App or T_Choice or T_TApp (with open_ty T' U' = Arrow X X — could happen if T' = Arrow (BVar 0) (BVar 0), i.e., another ∀Y. Y→Y). Hmm, so the "shape" isn't simply T_Abs. This makes a syntactic proof messy: we'd need an induction on typing derivations with a clever measure or a general statement about "terms of type T in context Gamma reduce to canonical form".

The logical relation handles all this uniformly. I think the logical relation is the way to go.

Let me now design the full proof carefully, keeping the syntactic lemmas minimal.

Simplification idea: instead of general type substitutions σ and term substitutions γ, use "simultaneous substitution" functions and prove the fundamental theorem directly. We need these syntactic operations and lemmas:

1. `ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) T` — substitute free atoms.
2. `tm_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (gamma : atom -> tm) t` — substitute free type atoms and free term atoms.

But actually, we can simplify: in the fundamental theorem, do we need general σ? The typing derivation mentions types; we need to instantiate free type variables. Since the theorem starts with Delta = [] and the only way Delta grows is T_TAbs (which introduces a fresh atom X and its type is opened), the types in the derivation are of the form "open_ty T (FVar X)" and the term is "open_tm_ty t (FVar X)"; then when we apply the substitution, we need to substitute X with a type A. Instead of a full σ, we could handle the fresh atoms one at a time with the existing `ty_subst`/`tm_ty_subst` operations. But then the fundamental theorem statement would be with a *single* atom substitution? Hmm, under nested T_TAbs we'd have multiple free atoms.

I think a general σ is cleaner, but we can implement `ty_apply` and `tm_apply` as fixpoints over the type/term structure:

```
Fixpoint ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_apply sigma T1) (ty_apply sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T => Ty_All (ty_apply sigma T)  (* note: sigma is total; bound BVars unaffected *)
  end.
```

Wait, but with de Bruijn indices and this non-shifting representation, substituting under Ty_All: the bound variable is BVar 0, and ty_apply doesn't touch BVars, so we're fine (no capture issue since atoms are distinct from BVars). ✓ And `open_ty (ty_apply sigma T) (sigma ... )`? Hmm, we need a commutation: ty_apply sigma (open_ty T U) = open_ty (ty_apply sigma T) (ty_apply sigma U). But open_ty_rec substitutes at BVar indices, so commuting should hold as long as sigma doesn't introduce BVars... Actually ty_apply sigma maps atoms to types which may contain BVars (e.g., in the T_TAbs case we substitute X := A where A is a closed type — no free BVars). To be safe, assume sigma maps to closed types (lc_ty_at 0). Then commutation should hold. Let's check: open_ty_rec k U T substitutes BVar k with U. ty_apply sigma distributes over the structure and maps atoms. Since sigma's values are closed, no capture issues. The equation ty_apply sigma (open_ty T U) = open_ty (ty_apply sigma T) (ty_apply sigma U) should hold by induction. ✓ (No shifting involved.)

But wait, there's a subtlety with `ty_subst` and the fundamental theorem at T_TAbs: the rule's premise uses `open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)` with X fresh; then when we apply sigma (which may map X to A), we need to relate `ty_apply (sigma[X:=A]) (open_ty T (FVar X))` to `open_ty (ty_apply (sigma[X:=A]) T) A`. By commutation: ty_apply (σ') (open_ty T (FVar X)) = open_ty (ty_apply σ' T) (ty_apply σ' (FVar X)) = open_ty (ty_apply σ' T) A. Note ty_apply σ' T = ty_apply σ T if X is fresh/not free in T (T's free vars are from Delta and X is fresh). Hmm, is X not free in T? T is the type in the conclusion `Ty_All T`; the type context was Delta, and T is the body which may have BVar 0 bound and free vars from Delta. X is fresh w.r.t. Delta (by the T_TAbs premise), so X is not free in T. ✓ so ty_apply σ' T = ty_apply σ T.

Similarly for terms: `tm_apply sigma gamma t`. And commutation with open_tm: tm_apply sigma gamma (open_tm t u) = open_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t) (tm_apply sigma gamma u)? Hmm, open_tm replaces tm_bvar 0 with u, and tm_apply doesn't touch tm_bvars, so yes by induction. ✓. And with open_tm_ty: tm_apply sigma gamma (open_tm_ty t U) = open_tm_ty (tm_apply sigma gamma t) (ty_apply sigma U)? Let's check: open_tm_ty_rec k U t replaces type BVar k with U inside types, incrementing k under tm_tabs. tm_apply substitutes type atoms (FVar) using sigma and term atoms (tm_fvar) using gamma; it doesn't touch BVars, so commutation should hold... but careful: when tm_apply descends under a tm_abs T t1, it applies ty_apply to T and tm_apply to t1 — same structure as open_tm_ty. ✓. And under tm_tabs, both increment k for types only. ✓.

Alright. Now, the value relation design. Let me carefully define things to make the fundamental theorem provable.

Let `R := tm -> tm -> Prop` (relations on terms). Define:

```
Definition value_rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.  (* or use R directly *)
```

We need the value interpretation:

```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => False
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      forall a1 a2, VR rho T1 a1 a2 ->
        exists b1 b2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* b1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* b2 /\ VR rho T2 b1 b2
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      forall A B, lc_ty_at 0 A -> lc_ty_at 0 B ->
        exists b1 b2, tm_tapp v1 A -->* b1 /\ tm_tapp v2 B -->* b2 /\
          VR (fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then R else rho Y) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) b1 b2
  end.
```

Hmm, the All case needs to extend rho at the *bound* variable, which after opening with FVar X becomes a free atom X. We need X to be fresh w.r.t. rho and T1's free variables. Let's instead define the All case by quantifying over an arbitrary relation and using a specific fresh atom. To avoid freshness, we can pick X = some fixed atom not free in anything? There's no guaranteed fresh atom. Alternative: use a *de Bruijn environment* for the relation: rho : nat -> rel indexed by de Bruijn level? Since the type has BVars for bound variables, and FVars for free ones. Define the interpretation with two environments: one for free atoms (rho : atom -> rel) and one for bound-variable depth (a list of relations for the enclosing binders). Then:

```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> rel) (xi : list rel) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i xi (fun _ _ => False) v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => ... VR rho xi T1 ... VR rho xi T2 ...
  | Ty_All T1 => value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      forall A B, lc_ty_at 0 A -> lc_ty_at 0 B ->
        exists b1 b2, tapp v1 A -->* b1 /\ tapp v2 B -->* b2 /\
          VR rho (R :: xi) T1 b1 b2
  end.
```

Oh nice — this avoids opening and freshness entirely! The bound variable index 0 refers to `nth 0 (R :: xi) = R`, and index i+1 refers to xi's entries. This matches the de Bruijn representation: inside Ty_All, the body's BVar 0 is the newly bound variable, and BVar i+1 refers to outer binders. But wait: `open_ty`-based typing rules use `open_ty T (FVar X)`, which replaces BVar 0 with FVar X in T — and the FVar X is then interpreted by rho. If instead we use the de Bruijn environment xi, then for a type `open_ty T (FVar X)` where X is a free atom, the BVar indices in T that referred to outer binders remain. Hmm, in the definition above, the All case uses `VR rho (R :: xi) T1` on the *unopened* T1. Good, no opening.

But then the substitution commutation: the judgment types in T_TAbs are `open_ty T (FVar X)`. We'd need to relate VR with environments to types of the form open_ty. Ugh, two representations again.

Alternative: define the interpretation on *closed-under-binders* types using an environment for both free atoms and bound variables. Hmm.

Alternatively, use the "opened" style with fresh atoms but parametrize over the atom and freshness conditions. Since the relational environment rho is only relevant at free atoms, and when we open the bound variable with a fresh atom X, we can just say: for all atoms X and all relations R, with X not free in anything, ... The freshness condition can be handled by quantifying over X and requiring `~ In X (free_ty_vars T1 ++ ...)`. But actually, since rho is a total function atom -> rel, and X could coincide with a free variable of the type (from Delta) — we need X fresh to avoid clashing. But note: T is the body of a `Ty_All` in a *well-formed* type relative to Delta. The bound variable (BVar 0) is distinct from free atoms (FVars), and opening T with a fresh atom X makes it an fvar. If X clashed with a free atom in T, then the interpretation of the opened type would incorrectly use rho X for both the bound variable and the other free variable. Since free atoms in T are from Delta and X is fresh w.r.t. Delta (from the T_TAbs premise), we're fine in the induction. But VR must be well-defined for all types, including potentially clashing ones; the All clause needs to choose X such that it doesn't clash with the free atoms of T1 — but rho is defined for all atoms, and we want to quantify over R for the bound variable.

Approach: define the All case by quantifying over a relation R and using a fresh atom chosen via a "free variables of T" list. Formally:

```
| Ty_All T1 =>
    value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    forall A B, lc_ty_at 0 A -> lc_ty_at 0 B ->
      exists b1 b2, tapp v1 A -->* b1 /\ tapp v2 B -->* b2 /\
        exists X, ~ In X (free_ty_vars T1) /\ 
          VR (fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then R else rho Y) (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) b1 b2
```

Hmm, the "exists X, ~ In X ..." with freshness is awkward but workable. Alternatively, since the interpretation is invariant under renaming of free variables? Hmm.

Better idea: use the de Bruijn environment xi for *bound* variables, and handle the opened types in the typing rules by a *substitution lemma* that converts `open_ty T (FVar X)` under rho to `T` under xi. I.e., prove: for well-formed contexts and fresh X, VR rho xi' (open_ty T (FVar X)) = VR (rho[X := R]) (R :: xi) T? Hmm, mixing.

Hmm, let me think about which representation makes the typing rules easiest.

The typing rules use:
- T_TAbs: from `has_type (X::Delta) Gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) (open_ty T (FVar X))` conclude `has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tabs t) (Ty_All T)`.
- T_TApp: from `has_type Delta Gamma t (Ty_All T)` and `wf_ty Delta U` conclude `has_type Delta Gamma (tm_tapp t U) (open_ty T U)`.

In the fundamental theorem, we interpret types with free atoms in Delta. The All clause: instantiate at types A, B (closed) and relation R for the bound variable. Alternatively, avoid opening in the All clause by adding the *bound relation* to the environment and defining the interpretation with a combined environment: `rho : atom -> rel` for free atoms, `xi : list rel` for binders, and then define the interpretation on the *opened* types by a lemma:

Lemma VR_open: for fresh X (not free in T) and any R:

Let's go with the xi (list of relations for de Bruijn bound vars) approach. Actually hmm, wait: maybe even simpler — since types in judgments are well-formed, we can interpret `Ty_BVar i` with xi. We can always convert (b) to the xi form with the bridge lemma. Let's define the bridge lemma properly:

Lemma VR_open_fvar: for all T, X, R, rho:
  ~ In X (free_ty T) ->
  (forall v1 v2, VR (extend rho X R) [] (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) v1 v2 <-> VR (extend rho X R) (R :: xi) T v1 v2)
Hmm, but the RHS has an arbitrary xi, and LHS's open_ty T (FVar X): the BVars in T (other than index 0) refer to ... if T has free BVars beyond 0 (from an enclosing binder), then open_ty T (FVar X) has BVar i for i≥1 which in the LHS is interpreted by xi at index i, while in the RHS it's xi at index... The RHS has xi = R::xi', so BVar i in T for i≥1 maps to nth (i-1)?? Hmm, wait. Let's carefully check the de Bruijn convention.

Type T with BVar 0 = innermost binder. `open_ty T U = open_ty_rec 0 U T` substitutes BVar 0 with U, and under a Ty_All it increments (open_ty_rec (S k) U T1). So BVar 0 is the innermost All binder. For the environment xi: xi is a list of relations in order: nth 0 xi = innermost binder's relation, nth 1 xi = next outer, etc.

Now consider T = open_ty T1 (FVar X) where T1 is the body of an All. T1 has BVar 0 = that All's binder, and BVar i (i≥1) = outer binders. After opening, FVar X replaces BVar 0, and BVar i in T (i≥1) are the outer binders — but in the opened type T, these BVars are "free" in T (not bound within T). Hmm, but if T1 had BVar 1 free (bound by an outer All), then in the whole type `Ty_All T1`, BVar 1 refers to the outer binder. So the bridge lemma with matching indices:

VR rho xi' (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) where xi' = xi (the outer env) equals VR (rho[X:=R]) (R :: xi) T1.

Yes: in the opened type, the free BVars i (i≥1) refer to outer binders indexed by xi at (i-1); in the unopened T1 with R::xi, BVar i (i≥1) maps to nth i (R::xi) = nth (i-1) xi. ✓. So the bridge is:

Lemma VR_open_ty: for all T1, X fresh w.r.t. free atoms of T1, R, rho, xi:
  VR (rho[X := R]) xi (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X)) v1 v2 <-> VR (rho[X := R]) (R :: xi) T1 v1 v2.

Proof by induction on T1. But T1 has BVar 0 in it at the top? In the induction, T1 can be any type; the lemma should be stated for arbitrary T and works by induction:
- T = Ty_BVar 0: LHS = VR rho' xi (open_ty (BVar 0) (FVar X)) = VR rho' xi (FVar X) = rho' X = R. RHS = VR rho' (R::xi) (BVar 0) = nth 0 (R::xi) = R. ✓

Now, crucially, the substitution/bridge lemma connects the judgment type `open_ty T U` (structural U) to the environment interpretation. Let's restate the unary relation with the environment design:

```
Definition Rel := tm -> Prop.

Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> Rel) (xi : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i xi (fun _ => False) v
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v /\
      forall a, VR rho xi T1 a ->
        exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ VR rho xi T2 b
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value v /\
      forall U, lc_ty_at 0 U ->
        forall S : Rel,
        exists b, tm_tapp v U -->* b /\ VR rho (S :: xi) T1 b
  end.
```

```
Fixpoint VR rho xi T v :=
```

OK here's where the type substitution and the environment need to be thought through carefully. Let's think about what the fundamental theorem should say, in the unary setting.

Standard unary parametricity (logical predicate) for System F: Use type substitutions σ and a predicate environment ρ where each free type variable X is interpreted by a predicate on values "of type σ X". The fundamental theorem: if Δ ⊢ t : T, then for all σ, ρ (with ρ X ⊆ values of σ X), and term environment γ mapping x:T' to values satisfying ⟦σT'⟧_ρ, we have ⟦σT⟧_ρ (t[σ,γ]).

The predicate ⟦T⟧_ρ is defined by recursion on T:
- ⟦FVar X⟧_ρ = ρ X.
- ⟦Arrow T1 T2⟧_ρ = { f : forall a ∈ ⟦T1⟧_ρ, f a ⇓ b ∈ ⟦T2⟧_ρ }
- ⟦All T1⟧_ρ = { v : for all U, S ⊆ values, with ... , v[U] ⇓ b ∈ ⟦T1⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} }.

The issue: in the All case, the relation S for X must be related to U somehow for the T_TApp case to work, where the type is `open_ty T1 U` (structural U, and X not free). But wait: in the unary setting with σ, at T_TApp the judgment type is `open_ty T U` where U is a type from the object language with free variables from Delta (interpreted by σ). The semantic interpretation ⟦σ(open_ty T U)⟧_ρ = ⟦open_ty (σ T) (σ U)⟧_ρ. Hmm, note σ distributes over open_ty (with fresh vars). We want to relate it to ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦S]} where S should be ⟦σU⟧_ρ. So the All clause's S is not arbitrary — it must be ⟦σU⟧_ρ. But then the All clause would be: for all U, S with S = ⟦U⟧_ρ... but ⟦U⟧_ρ depends on ρ and U's free variables. Let's now write the fundamental theorem:

```
Theorem fundamental :
  forall Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma rho gamma,
    (* sigma: atom -> ty maps type variables to closed types *)
    (forall X, In X Delta -> lc_ty_at 0 (sigma X)) ->
    (* gamma: atom -> tm maps term variables to values *)
    (forall x T', lookup_context x Gamma = Some T' -> 
        VR rho [] (ty_apply sigma T') (gamma x)) ->
    locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t) /\
    TR rho [] (ty_apply sigma T) (tm_apply sigma gamma t).
```

where TR rho xi T t := exists v, t -->* v /\ VR rho xi T v.

I think the cleanest standard formulation is: the fundamental theorem is stated for a type T (with free atoms from Delta) and two environments: σ (type substitution) for the term side, and ρ (relation env) for the type side, with an invariant linking them... which we established can't hold for arbitrary S. Hmm.

Let's look at this from the standard "logical predicate" literature for System F. In a logical predicate interpretation, you interpret each type variable X by an arbitrary predicate on *terms* of the type σ X, but there's no requirement that the predicate be exactly ⟦σX⟧. The T_TAbs/T_TApp cases work as follows: 
- T_TAbs: to show tabs t ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧, for arbitrary type A and predicate S (on terms of type A-ish), show t[open with X] ∈ ⟦T⟧_{X↦S} where X is a fresh *term-level type variable* whose type substitution is A. So the environment is: σ(X) = A, ρ(X) = S, with S and A unrelated.
- T_TApp: t : ∀X.T, U a type. IH: t ∈ ⟦∀X.T⟧_ρ. Instantiate with A := σU and S := ⟦σU⟧_ρ (the structural interpretation). Then t[σU] ∈ ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦⟦σU⟧]}. And the conclusion type is open_ty T U, interpreted as ⟦σ(open_ty T U)⟧_ρ. Need: ⟦T⟧_{ρ[X↦⟦σU⟧]} = ⟦σ(open_ty T U)⟧_ρ. This is the "substitution lemma" and holds because both sides interpret U structurally.

So the invariant ρ and σ are unrelated *except* at the T_TApp use, where S is chosen as the structural interpretation of σU. And in T_TAbs, S is arbitrary. Good — this is consistent: the fundamental theorem doesn't need any invariant linking ρ and σ, only that ρ's predicates contain only values (for stepping) — or better, we build value-ness into VR.

But then, at T_Var: the variable x has type T', and the environment condition is: γ x ∈ ⟦σ T'⟧_ρ. Note σT' is closed and ⟦·⟧ is structural, so this uses ρ for... wait, σT' is a closed type (no free atoms), so ⟦σT'⟧_ρ doesn't use ρ. ✓. So the environment condition is ρ-independent. And at T_Var, we conclude VR rho xi (σ T') (γ x) — hmm, but the fundamental theorem's conclusion should be about the type T under σ AND ρ. Since σT' is closed, ρ is irrelevant. So the fundamental theorem can conclude VR rho [] (σ T) (term) — but then in T_TAbs, the premise's type σ'(open_ty T (FVar X)) = open_ty (σT) A is closed and doesn't mention X, so the IH gives VR [] (open_ty (σT) A) — but we need VR (S::[]) (σT) for arbitrary S, which is NOT the same. Contradiction!

Argh. The issue: at T_TAbs, the semantic interpretation needed is of the *open* type `open_ty T (FVar X)` where X is a free atom interpreted by S, not the substituted closed type `open_ty (σT) A`.

So the fundamental theorem must interpret types using the *open* form: the premise's type is `open_ty T (FVar X)` and its interpretation is VR rho' [] (open_ty T (FVar X)) where rho' X = S. So the interpretation should NOT apply σ to the type; rather, interpret T syntactically with rho for atoms, and σ is only used to compute the term's runtime type (which only matters for lc/stepping of type applications). But VR's All clause applies type application at a concrete type A, and the resulting term's type is `open_ty T A` — where the type substitution's image appears structurally.

OK so here's the crux: we need a relation VR rho xi T v where T is a type with free atoms (interpreted by rho) and free BVars (interpreted by xi), and the term's lc-ness/stepping uses the "runtime types" which are... hmm, wait. In the All clause, the step `tm_tapp v A -->* b` uses a type A. The body's type should be `open_ty T A` — but T here is the *syntactic body type* of the closed type? Hmm, let's think again about what the All clause should be:

VR rho xi (Ty_All T1) v := value v /\ forall A (lc), forall S, exists b, tapp v A -->* b /\ VR rho (S::xi) T1 b.

Here the type A is the runtime instantiation type, and the body type T1 is interpreted with S for the bound variable. Fine, no need for `open_ty` here. The IH (from the premise) is about the type σ'(open_ty T1 (FVar X)) = open_ty (σT1) A — a different type (opened with A, not σT1 with S).

Hmm. So we need a bridge between VR (S::[]) (σT1) and VR [] (open_ty (σT1) A). This bridge would require S = ⟦A⟧, but S is arbitrary. So σ-application to the type is incompatible with arbitrary S at T_TAbs. Conclusion: we should NOT σ-apply types in the interpretation. Instead, the fundamental theorem should interpret the *original* type T with rho for its free atoms, where the free atoms include the ones introduced by T_TAbs (opened with FVar X). But wait: the term side's runtime types come from σ; and the interpretation of types must match: in T_TApp, the term is `tapp t U`; the All clause instantiates at runtime type A := σU, and the body interpretation is VR rho (S::[]) (body type) with S := ??? For the conclusion, the judgment type is `open_ty T U`, interpreted with rho as VR rho [] (open_ty T U). This is the bridge lemma in the other direction: opening with the free atom X assigned S equals the de Bruijn environment push S. ✓ This is exactly the bridge theorem stated earlier (with the free atom X, fresh, assigned S):

VR rho' [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) <-> VR rho' (S :: []) T1, where rho' = rho[X:=S].

Hmm, but note the RHS's rho' is irrelevant for the free atom X? The RHS type T1 may contain other free atoms (from Delta) interpreted by rho' — and rho' agrees with rho on them. But T1 also contains BVar 0 (the bound variable) interpreted by S. And on the LHS, `open_ty T1 (FVar X)` has X interpreted by rho' X = S ✓. So the bridge holds. But note the LHS's rho' is rho[X:=S] and the RHS's is also rho[X:=S] — and since X ∉ free(T1) (X fresh), the RHS doesn't consult rho' at X anyway. So the bridge is: VR (rho[X:=S]) [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) <-> VR rho (S::[]) T1. ✓.

So we need the bridge lemma in this form:
For all T1 (lc_ty_at 1), X fresh w.r.t. T1, S, rho:
  VR (rho[X:=S]) [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) v <-> VR rho (S::[]) T1 v.

And its generalization for the T_TApp case:
For all T1 (lc_ty_at 1), U (lc_ty_at 0), rho:
  VR rho [fun w => VR rho [] U w] T1 v <-> VR rho [] (open_ty T1 U) v.

Hmm, wait: careful about the environments in the second bridge. On the LHS, the environment has one entry S = ⟦U⟧_rho. On the RHS, `open_ty T1 U` is interpreted with env [] and rho. Let's verify with T1 = Ty_All T2 (the shifting case): 
LHS = VR rho [⟦U⟧] (Ty_All T2) v = value v /\ forall A S', exists b, tapp v A -->* b /\ VR rho (S'::[⟦U⟧]) T2 b.
RHS = VR rho [] (Ty_All (open_ty_rec 1 U T2)) v = value v /\ forall A S', exists b, tapp v A -->* b /\ VR rho (S'::[]) (open_ty_rec 1 U T2) b.
So we need VR rho (S'::[⟦U⟧]) T2 b <-> VR rho (S'::[]) (open_ty_rec 1 U T2) b. This is the general bridge at depth 1: substituting U at index 1 (the free var one out) corresponds to the environment having ⟦U⟧ at position 1. So generalize:

Lemma VR_open_general: for all k, T, U, rho, (env : list Rel) with length env = k:
  lc_ty_at (S k) T -> lc_ty_at 0 U ->

Syntactic approach outline:
1. Define `norm` or "reduces to canonical form" predicates for terms of a given type in a given context.
2. Key syntactic lemma: if `has_type [X] (x:X) body X` (with X a free type variable and x the only term variable of type X), then for any closed U, value v : U: `body[U][x:=v] -->* v`.

Let's think about a general syntactic lemma: For any derivation `has_type Delta Gamma body X` where X is a type variable (a free atom, i.e., X ∈ Delta? not necessarily—X could be any atom), and Gamma has all variable types being... hmm.

I think the syntactic approach requires an induction over typing derivations with a strong statement like: "for all terms t of type T in context Gamma, either t diverges or reduces to a value of type T" — which is essentially strong normalization + progress, requiring a logical relation anyway (or a reducibility candidate argument). System F strong normalization is nontrivial. So the logical relation is the way.

OK let's commit to the logical relation approach. Let me carefully write out all the pieces and lemmas. I'll aim for a clean, minimal infrastructure.

### Core definitions

```coq
Definition Rel := tm -> Prop.

(* free type variables of a type, as a Prop *)
Fixpoint free_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => free_ty T1 ++ free_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => free_ty T1
  end.
```

Also `free_ty_tm : tm -> list atom` for terms? We may need it for rho-irrelevance in the term side? Hmm, maybe not.

Type substitution:
```coq
Fixpoint ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_apply sigma T1) (ty_apply sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_apply sigma T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_apply sigma T) (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (ty_apply sigma T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  end.
```

Wait: `tm_bvar i` is a bound *term* variable (de Bruijn); substituting free term variables (atoms) doesn't touch them. But if a tm_bvar is free (index relative to binder depth), we'd need to handle it — but typable terms have none. OK.

Hmm, actually should `tm_apply` also count term-variable binder depth for tm_bvar? Not needed since we substitute atoms.

Now the value relation:

```coq
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> Rel) (xi : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => value v /\ (nth i xi (fun _ => False)) v
  | Ty_FVar X => value v /\ rho X v
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v /\
      (forall a, VR rho xi T1 a ->
         exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ VR rho xi T2 b)
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      value v /\
      (forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A ->
       forall S, exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\ VR rho (S :: xi) T1 b)
  end.
```

Hmm, at Ty_BVar, requiring value v might be problematic if the environment relation xi's entry doesn't imply value — but we add `value v` ourselves so the invariant "VR implies value" holds at every type. ✓. But then in the T_TAbs case, proving VR (S::[]) T1 where T1's BVar 0 occurs, we need `value b` for the result of the term — which should hold since the term reduces to a value (from TR's definition) and VR at the premise gives value. ✓.

Wait, careful: in the All clause, the environment xi grows by S. If the body T1 is itself an All, the clause pushes S' etc. So the environment xi grows unboundedly inside nested Alls — fine, it's a Coq list. Note: since VR is a Fixpoint on T, and the All clause recurses on T1 (a strict subterm), this is structurally decreasing ✓. But the environment grows; the recursion is on T only ✓.

Now, is `VR rho xi T` well-defined for types with free BVars? Yes, via xi.

### The term relation

```coq
Definition TR (rho : atom -> Rel) (xi : list Rel) (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v, t -->* v /\ VR rho xi T v.
```

### Environment for the fundamental theorem

The fundamental theorem:

```coq
Theorem fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma rho gamma,
    (forall X, In X Delta -> lc_ty_at 0 (sigma X)) ->
    (forall x T', lookup_context x Gamma = Some T' -> TR?? ...)
```

Hmm, the term environment condition: for each variable x with type T', we need VR at the *interpreted type* of T'. But which type? The judgment type T' is a syntactic type with free atoms in Delta. The term `gamma x` is a runtime value whose type is `sigma T'`. The semantic interpretation should be VR rho [] (sigma T') (gamma x)? But then the free atoms in T' get replaced by sigma, and rho isn't used. But the fundamental theorem's conclusion interprets T with rho... Hmm, inconsistent!

Let's re-think. In the earlier analysis, I concluded: the type interpretation should NOT σ-apply; it should interpret the syntactic type T under rho. But then at T_Var with type T' (a Gamma type), the environment condition should be VR rho [] T' (gamma x) — the syntactic type T' under rho. But the runtime term gamma x has type sigma T'; is it guaranteed to be in the interpretation? The environment condition *assumes* it. So the fundamental theorem's hypothesis: for all x T', lookup x Gamma = Some T' -> VR rho [] T' (gamma x). Hmm, but then at T_Abs, we extend Gamma with (x, T1) and need VR rho [] T1 a for the argument a — we'd need to provide this from the Arrow interpretation's premise: VR rho xi T1 a ✓ (the Arrow clause quantifies over a with VR rho xi T1 a). But the IH is used with the extended Gamma and the environment condition for the new variable (x, T1): VR rho [] T1 a — matching if T1 is closed w.r.t. BVars (it is, since it's wf in Delta... well, T1 is wf in Delta and has no free BVars ✓, but xi vs []: VR rho [] T1 vs VR rho xi T1 — the type T1 has no free BVars, so the xi is irrelevant (need lemma: lc_ty_at 0 T -> VR rho xi T v <-> VR rho [] T v; hmm, actually lc_ty_at 0 T means no free BVars ✓). But wait: T1 may be an All type, and inside, xi grows via pushes; the presence of an initial prefix doesn't matter since no BVar refers to it (indices within T1 are internal and get bound by internal Alls) — hmm, careful: if T1 = Ty_All T2 with lc_ty_at 0 T1, then lc_ty_at 1 T2, and inside, BVar 0 refers to the pushed S, and any BVar i refers to internal binders; the initial xi is at positions ≥ depth, never consulted since lc bounds the free indices. ✓ Need a lemma: lc_ty_at (length xi) T -> VR rho xi T v <-> VR rho [] T v? Hmm, more precisely: if all free BVars of T are < length xi... no. Let's think: VR rho xi T consults xi at Ty_BVar i. If T has lc_ty_at 0 (no free BVars), then at any depth d, the BVars occurring have i < d (bound by enclosing internal binders), and the environment at that point is (pushed relations) ++ xi with length d + length xi; nth i of it for i < d is from the pushed part ✓, so xi is never consulted. So: lc_ty_at 0 T -> VR rho xi1 T v <-> VR rho xi2 T v. Provable by induction on T (the All case: body has lc_ty_at 1, push the same S on both sides, then use the IH for lc_ty_at 1 with environments S::xi1 and S::xi2 — hmm, the IH needs "all free BVars < 1", which means xi is consulted at index... hmm, with lc_ty_at 1, index 0 is free and would be consulted, and it's at position 0 in both S::xi1 and S::xi2 (the same S) ✓. So generalize: lc_ty_at k T -> for all xi1 xi2 agreeing on the first k entries, VR rho xi1 T v <-> VR rho xi2 T v. Where "agreeing on the first k entries" means for all i < k, nth i xi1 = nth i xi2 (or pointwise iff). This is provable by induction. Alternatively, simpler: prove the combined lemma for lc_ty_at (length xi) with... hmm.

Simpler alternative: avoid needing these lemmas by making xi a *fixed-length* environment? Hmm.

Alternatively: define VR with xi as a *function* nat -> Rel and require lc-ness... same issue.

Let's just prove the general "environment irrelevance" lemma:

Lemma VR_env_agree : forall T rho xi1 xi2 v,
  lc_ty_at (length xi1) T ->
  (forall i, i < length xi1 -> forall u, nth i xi1 (fun _=>False) u <-> nth i xi2 (fun _=>False) u) ->
  (VR rho xi1 T v <-> VR rho xi2 T v).

Hmm, length conditions. Alternatively define the environment as a list and require xi1 and xi2 to be equal on the relevant prefix. Actually simplest: prove `VR rho xi T v` depends only on `first (number of free BVars) xi`. Hmm.

Actually, here's a MUCH simpler approach to environments: since judgment types are wf (no free BVars), and the only place we push relations is the All clause, maybe use the following alternative definition that avoids xi entirely by *substituting at the type level with fresh atoms chosen canonically*... no, we discussed.

Hmm, hold on. Let's reconsider. What if we define the All clause as:

```
| Ty_All T1 =>
    value v /\
    forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A ->
    forall S, exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\
       VR rho xi (open_ty T1 (Ty_FVar X_fresh?)) ...
```
No.

Alternative: define VR by recursion on T with an environment xi, but *always* call it with xi = [] for judgment types, and use the bridge lemma for the All case. Specifically:

- In the fundamental theorem, conclude VR rho [] T (term) for the judgment type T (closed w.r.t. BVars).
- T_TAbs: the goal is VR rho [] (Ty_All T1) (tabs s). Unfolds to: forall A S, exists b, tapp (tabs s) A -->* b /\ VR rho [S] T1 b. Use the IH from the premise: VR rho' [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) (open_tm_ty s A) with rho' = rho[X:=S]. The bridge (U := FVar X, k=0): VR rho' [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) <-> VR rho' [rho' X] T1 = VR rho' [S] T1. Then rho-irrelevance: X not free in T1 -> VR rho' [S] T1 <-> VR rho [S] T1. ✓ So the conclusion VR rho [S] T1 holds.
- T_TApp: the goal is VR rho [] (open_ty T U) (tapp s (sigma U)). Hmm, wait, the term is `tapp s A` where A = sigma U (the runtime type). The judgment type is `open_ty T U` — under rho (syntactic U). The IH gives VR rho [] (Ty_All T) s. Unfolds: forall A lc, forall S, exists b, tapp s A -->* b /\ VR rho [S] T b. Take A := sigma U (need lc_ty_at 0 (sigma U) — from wf_ty Delta U + sigma closed ✓). Take S := fun w => VR rho [] U w (the interpretation of U under rho). Then get b with tapp s (sigma U) -->* b and VR rho [⟦U⟧] T b. By the bridge (in reverse), VR rho [] (open_ty T U) b ✓.

So the environment xi is needed only in the bridge, and judgment types are always interpreted with xi = []. Then the "environment irrelevance" lemma: in T_TAbs we need VR rho' [S] T1 <-> VR rho [S] T1 where X ∉ free(T1). That's a rho-irrelevance lemma (not a xi one). And in the fundamental theorem's other cases (T_Abs etc.), we call VR with xi = [] consistently. ✓ This seems simpler. The bridge lemma needs a general env (for the nested All case inside the induction), but that's internal to the bridge proof.

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
```

Great. Now the remaining question: the environment condition's exact form. We need: `forall x T', lookup_context x Gamma = Some T' -> VR rho [] T' (gamma x)`. Note this includes value-ness/lc of gamma x ✓.

At T_Abs, we extend Gamma with (x, T1), gamma with x ↦ a, and need: for all y T', lookup y (x|->T1;Gamma) = Some T' -> VR rho [] T' (gamma' y). The IH's environment condition requires this. So we need VR rho [] T1 a ✓ (given by the Arrow clause). For y ≠ x: lookup y (x|->T1;Gamma) = lookup y Gamma (since x ≠ y, the eqb is false) ✓, and gamma' y = gamma y ✓ (assuming the update is by eqb). ✓.

OK. Now, the other subtlety: in T_Abs, the IH is applied at the fresh x with gamma' = gamma[x:=a] where a is the argument; but we need the environment condition for the *whole* extended Gamma. ✓ as shown.

Now, what about the "locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t)" in the fundamental theorem — we decided to prove it as a separate lemma. But there's a catch: the separate lc lemma's hypothesis is `forall x, locally_closed_tm (gamma x)` — for ALL atoms x, not just those in Gamma. That's fine (at the call sites, gamma maps everything to lc). But in T_Abs, extending gamma[x:=a] requires a lc — we can choose any lc term a (e.g., `tm_abs (Ty_FVar 0) (tm_bvar 0)`) since the lc lemma doesn't care about the type interpretation ✓. 

So the lc lemma:
```
Lemma lc_subst_tm : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma gamma,
    (forall X, In X Delta -> lc_ty_at 0 (sigma X)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed_tm (gamma x)) ->
    locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t).
```
Proof by induction on the derivation. Cases:
- T_Var: gamma x is lc ✓.
- T_Abs: need lc_tm_at 0 0 (abs (σT1) body) = lc_ty_at 0 (σT1) ∧ lc_tm_at 0 1 body. lc_ty_at 0 (σT1) from wf_ty Delta T1 + sigma hypothesis (lemma wf_lc_subst). For lc_tm_at 0 1 body: apply IH to the premise with x chosen appropriately and gamma' = gamma[x:=a] where a is any lc term. The IH gives locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma' (open_tm t2 (FVar x))) = locally_closed_tm (open_tm body a) (commutation). Then invert open_tm ✓.
  Hmm, the IH's hypothesis `forall y, locally_closed_tm (gamma' y)`: for y = x it's a ✓; for y ≠ x it's gamma y ✓.
- T_App: lc_app inversion: IH gives lc of both subterms; the conclusion term is app (sub1) (sub2) ✓.
- T_TAbs: need lc_tm_at 1 0 (tm_apply sigma gamma t) from the IH on the premise: lc of open_tm_ty (tm_apply sigma gamma t) A where σ' = σ[X:=A] and A is a closed type. Wait, what should A be? The IH requires `forall X, In X (X0::Delta) -> lc_ty_at 0 (sigma' X)`; for X0 we need lc_ty_at 0 A, so choose A := any closed type, e.g., Ty_FVar 0 (lc via lc_ty_fvar) ✓ or Ty_All... Let's use `Ty_FVar 0`. Then the IH gives lc of open_tm_ty (tm_apply sigma gamma t) A; invert the type-opening ✓.
  But hold on: the premise term is `open_tm_ty t (Ty_FVar X0)` where X0 is the fresh atom. And σ' = σ[X0 := A]. Then tm_apply σ' gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X0)) = open_tm_ty (tm_apply sigma gamma t) A by commutation ✓.
- T_TApp: term = tapp t U. lc = lc of tm_apply sigma gamma t (IH) ∧ lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma U) (from wf_ty Delta U) ✓.
- T_Choice: lc of both IH subterms ✓.

So the lc lemma needs: wf_lc_subst (wf_ty Delta T -> sigma closed on Delta -> lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma T)), open_tm inversion, open_tm_ty inversion, and the two commutation lemmas. Manageable.

So let's define:
```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> Rel) (env : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i env (fun _ => False) v
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      forall a, value a -> VR rho env T1 a ->
        exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho env T2 b
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A -> forall S,
        exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho (S :: env) T1 b
  end.

Definition VRV rho env T v := value v /\ VR rho env T v.
```
Hmm, but then the Arrow clause needs `value a` explicitly (since VR T1 a doesn't imply value a). And the result needs value b explicitly. Similarly for All. This is workable. But the T_TAbs case: VRV rho env (All T1) v = value v ∧ forall A lc S, exists b, tapp v A -->* b ∧ value b ∧ VR rho (S::env) T1 b. Then in the All clause (used in the free theorem), we need VRV for the argument: given VRV rho [S] (BVar 0) a = value a ∧ S a ✓.

Hmm, but the Arrow clause uses `VR rho env T1 a` (without value), while the caller provides VRV = value ∧ VR. So we pass both. And in the free theorem: b v -->* c with value c ∧ VR rho [S] (BVar 0) c = S c. With S = fun a => a = v, we get c = v ✓.

And the fundamental theorem's environment condition: VRV rho [] T' (gamma x) (value + VR).

Hmm, but now the bridge lemma's statement becomes: VRV rho (env++[⟦U⟧]) T v <-> VRV rho env (open_ty_rec k U T) v where ⟦U⟧ = fun w => VR rho [] U w. Since both sides have value v ∧ ..., and the bridge reduces to the inner VR bridge ✓. So prove the inner VR bridge (without value) and derive VRV.

But wait, there's a subtlety: the bridge's inner-VR statement at BVar k: LHS = nth k (env++[⟦U⟧]) v = VR rho [] U v; RHS = VR rho env U v. So we need VR rho env U v <-> VR rho [] U v for lc_ty_at 0 U ✓ (env-irrelevance).

OK, let's define:
```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> Rel) (env : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i env (fun _ => False) v
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      forall a, VRV rho env T1 a ->
        exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ VRV rho env T2 b
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A -> forall S,
        exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\ VRV rho (S :: env) T1 b
  end
with VRV (rho : atom -> Rel) (env : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  value v /\ VR rho env T v.
```
Coq allows mutually recursive Fixpoints with `with`. ✓ Or define VRV after VR and inline `value a /\ VR ...` in the clauses. Since Fixpoint can't reference a later definition, use mutual fixpoint or inline `value a /\ VR rho env T1 a`. Inlining is simpler: 
```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall a, value a /\ VR rho env T1 a -> exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho env T2 b
| Ty_All T1 => forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A -> forall S, exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho (S::env) T1 b
```
Good, no mutual recursion.

So define:
```
Fixpoint VR (rho : atom -> Rel) (env : list Rel) (T : ty) (v : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => nth i env (fun _ => False) v
  | Ty_FVar X => rho X v
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      forall a, value a /\ VR rho env T1 a ->
        exists b, tm_app v a -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho env T2 b
  | Ty_All T1 =>
      forall A, lc_ty_at 0 A -> forall S,
        exists b, tm_tapp v A -->* b /\ value b /\ VR rho (S :: env) T1 b
  end.
Definition VRV rho env T v := value v /\ VR rho env T v.
```

  VR rho (S::[]) T1 b': from VRV rho' [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) b' we have VR rho' [] (open_ty T1 (FVar X)) b'.   Now the env entry: `fun w => VR rho' [] (FVar X) w` is convertible to `fun w => rho' X w`; and rho' X = update X S rho X = if X =? X then S else rho X. We want to convert to [S]. Use VR_env_agree with env1 := [fun w => VR rho' [] (FVar X) w] and env2 := [S], requiring length equality ✓ and pointwise iff on the first 1: for u, (fun w => VR rho' [] (FVar X) w) u <-> S u. Unfolding VR at FVar: it's rho' X u <-> S u, and rho' X u = (if X =? X then S else rho X) u = S u by eqb_refl ✓. So VR rho' [S] T1 b' ✓.
  Then VR_rho_agree: X ∉ free_ty T1 → VR rho [S] T1 b' ✓.
  Done ✓.

Phew. OK.

Now let's reconsider the T_TApp case in detail:
Goal: TR rho [] (open_ty T U) (tm_app (tm_apply sigma gamma t)?? no: the term is tm_tapp t U, so tm_apply gives tm_tapp (tm_apply sigma gamma t) (ty_apply sigma U)).
IH: TR rho [] (Ty_All T) (tm_apply sigma gamma t) → exists v1, s -->* v1 ∧ VRV rho [] (Ty_All T) v1.
VRV = value v1 ∧ VR rho [] (All T) v1 = forall A lc, forall S, exists b, tapp v1 A -->* b ∧ value b ∧ VR rho (S::[]) T b.
Take A := ty_apply sigma U. lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma U): from wf_ty Delta U and sigma lc-all ✓ (wf_lc_ty_apply).
Take S := fun w => VR rho [] U w.
Get b: tapp v1 (ty_apply sigma U) -->* b, value b, VR rho [S] T b.
Multi-step: tm_tapp s (ty_apply sigma U) -->* tm_tapp v1 (ty_apply sigma U) via multi_tapp with lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma U) ✓, then -->* b ✓.
Bridge (k=0, env=[], U syntactic): VR rho ([] ++ [fun w => VR rho [] U w]) T b <-> VR rho [] (open_ty_rec 0 U T) b, requires lc_ty_at 1 T and lc_ty_at 0 U ✓. Note [] ++ [S] = [S] ✓ definitionally (nil ++ l = l ✓).
So VR rho [] (open_ty T U) b ✓. And value b ✓. So VRV rho [] (open_ty T U) b ✓.
TR ✓ with the composed multi-step.

lc_ty_at 1 T: from the "judgment types are lc" lemma applied to the premise `has_type Delta Gamma t (Ty_All T)`: gives lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) = lc_ty_at 1 T ✓.
lc_ty_at 0 U: from wf_ty Delta U ✓ (wf_ty_lc).

Great.

Now the T_Abs case in detail:
Goal: TR rho [] (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (tm_abs (ty_apply sigma T1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)).
Take v := that abs value; 0 steps.
VRV = value (abs ...) ∧ VR rho [] (Arrow T1 T2) (abs ...).
- value: lc of the abs: from the lc lemma at the T_Abs judgment ✓ (requires sigma lc-all, gamma lc-all).
- VR: forall a, value a ∧ VR rho [] T1 a -> exists b, app (abs ...) a -->* b ∧ value b ∧ VR rho [] T2 b.
  Given a with value a and VR rho [] T1 a. Choose a fresh x ∉ L ∪ free_tm_tm t2. Apply the IH at x with gamma' := gamma[x:=a], sigma, rho (unchanged). Need:
  - sigma lc ✓.
  - environment condition: forall y T', lookup y (x|->T1;Gamma) = Some T' -> VRV rho [] T' (gamma' y).
    - If y = x: lookup x (x|->T1;Gamma) = Some T1 (by lookup definition: eqb x x = true; need to show with Nat.eqb_refl ✓). So T' = T1 and gamma' x = a; VRV rho [] T1 a ✓ (given).
    - If y ≠ x: lookup y (x|->T1;Gamma) = lookup y Gamma (by eqb y x = false ✓); gamma' y = gamma y ✓; use the outer condition ✓.
  - gamma' lc-all: for y = x, a is a value → lc ✓; else gamma y ✓.
  IH: TR rho [] T2 (tm_apply sigma gamma' (open_tm t2 (FVar x))).
  Rewrite the term to open_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t2) a via the commutation lemma + freshness of x w.r.t. free_tm_tm t2 ✓.
  Unfold TR: exists b, open_tm body a -->* b ∧ VRV rho [] T2 b.
  Step: app (abs (σT1) body) a --> open_tm body a (ST_AppAbs: locally_closed_tm (abs ...) ✓ value-ness, value a ✓), then -->* b ✓.
  VRV T2 b ✓.
  Done.

Now the lc lemma (lc_subst_tm). Let me re-examine its statement and proof:

```
Lemma lc_subst_tm : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma gamma,
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sigma X)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed_tm (gamma x)) ->
    locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma t).
```
Proof by induction on has_type.
- T_Var: gamma x lc ✓.
- T_Abs: need locally_closed_tm (abs (ty_apply sigma T1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)) = lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma T1) ∧ lc_tm_at 0 1 (tm_apply sigma gamma t2).
  - lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma T1) from wf_lc_ty_apply with wf_ty Delta T1 ✓ and sigma lc-all ✓.
  - lc_tm_at 0 1 (...): apply the IH to the premise with a fresh x and gamma' := gamma[x := c] where c is some lc term (e.g., tm_abs (Ty_FVar 0) (tm_bvar 0)? that's lc ✓). The IH gives locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma' (open_tm t2 (FVar x))) = locally_closed_tm (open_tm (tm_apply sigma gamma' t2) c). By freshness of x (choose x ∉ free_tm_tm t2), tm_apply sigma gamma' t2 = tm_apply sigma gamma t2. So lc_tm_at 0 0 (open_tm body c). Then the inversion lemma gives lc_tm_at 0 1 body ✓.
- T_App: IH gives lc of each; lc_tm_app ✓.
- T_TAbs: need locally_closed_tm (tabs (tm_apply sigma gamma t)) = lc_tm_at 1 0 (tm_apply sigma gamma t). Apply the IH to the premise at a fresh X with sigma' := sigma[X := cty] where cty is some lc type, e.g., Ty_FVar 0. IH: locally_closed_tm (tm_apply sigma' gamma (open_tm_ty t (FVar X))) = locally_closed_tm (open_tm_ty (tm_apply sigma gamma t) cty) (needs X ∉ free_ty_tm t ✓ choose fresh, and ty_apply sigma' (FVar X) = cty ✓). Then invert the type-opening: lc_tm_at 0 0 (open_tm_ty_rec 0 cty body) → lc_tm_at 1 0 body ✓ (type inversion lemma with matching indices 0,0 ✓).
- T_TApp: lc_tm_tapp: IH gives lc of the function; lc_ty_at 0 (ty_apply sigma U) from wf_lc_ty_apply with wf_ty Delta U ✓.
- T_Choice: lc_tm_choice ✓.

Note: the IH must be applicable for all sigma, gamma, so the induction hypothesis is general ✓ (we do induction on the derivation with sigma/gamma universally quantified — in Coq, `induction 1` gives IHs with the same universally quantified variables if we generalize properly. Since the statement has `forall sigma gamma` after `has_type`, we can do `intros ... H; induction H; intros sigma gamma Hsig Hgam`.)

```
Lemma has_type_lc_ty : forall Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T -> lc_ty_at 0 T.
```

- T_Abs: lc_ty_arrow with lc_ty_at 0 T1 (wf_ty_lc) and IH ✓.
- T_App: IH ✓.
- T_TAbs: need lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) = lc_ty_at 1 T. From the IH at a fresh X: lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T (FVar X)); invert ✓. Need to choose X ∉ L ✓ (fresh function).
- T_TApp: need lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T U). From the IH: lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) → lc_ty_at 1 T. And lc_ty_at 0 U from wf_ty_lc ✓. Then lc_ty_open (forward) gives lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T U) ✓.
- T_Choice: IH ✓.

```
Lemma wf_ty_lc : forall Delta T, wf_ty Delta T -> lc_ty_at 0 T.
```

```
Lemma wf_lc_ty_apply : forall Delta T, wf_ty Delta T -> forall sigma,

```

Then invert → lc_ty_at 1 (ty_apply sigma T); then lc_ty_all ✓.

And ty_apply commutation:
```
Lemma ty_apply_open : forall sigma k U T,
  ty_apply sigma (open_ty_rec k U T) = open_ty_rec k (ty_apply sigma U) (ty_apply sigma T).
```
Proof by induction on T, generalizing k ✓.

Also needed:
- `ty_apply_agree : (forall X, In X (free_ty T) -> sigma1 X = sigma2 X) -> ty_apply sigma1 T = ty_apply sigma2 T`.
- `tm_apply_agree : (forall X, In X (free_ty_tm t) -> sigma1 X = sigma2 X) -> (forall x, In x (free_tm_tm t) -> gamma1 x = gamma2 x) -> tm_apply sigma1 gamma1 t = tm_apply sigma2 gamma2 t`.

Definitions of free_ty, free_ty_tm, free_tm_tm:
```
Fixpoint free_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => free_ty T1 ++ free_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => free_ty T1
  end.

Fixpoint free_ty_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => free_ty T ++ free_ty_tm t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty_tm t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => free_ty_tm t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty_tm t2
  end.

Fixpoint free_tm_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs _ t1 => free_tm_tm t1
  | ...
```
Wait, really? For bound terms, the tm_bvar is a de Bruijn index, not an atom, so "free term atoms" are just the free fvars ✓. Fine.

Hmm, but careful with `tm_apply_open_tm` commutation: it substitutes gamma at fvars, including fvars that are "free" in the term. In the commutation, `tm_apply sigma gamma (open_tm_rec k u t)`: open_tm_rec replaces bvar k with u; tm_apply then substitutes fvars in both the result of the opening and in u. Both sides: open_tm_rec k (tm_apply sigma gamma u) (tm_apply sigma gamma t) ✓. No capture issues since atoms vs BVars are distinct ✓.

Now `fresh_atom`. Define:
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with [] => 0 | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l') end.
Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).
Lemma fresh_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Lemma fresh_notin_app : ...
```
Or simpler: use a list `l` and define fresh via `fold_right Nat.max 0 l + 1`. Then `fresh_notin` proven by induction using max properties (Nat.max_l, etc. from PeanoNat / Lia). Since atoms are nat and we have Lia, easy:
```
Lemma max_list_ge : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Lemma fresh_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof. intros l H. apply max_list_ge in H. unfold fresh in H. lia. Qed.
```

Also, in T_Abs/T_TAbs cases, we need to pick fresh atoms w.r.t. multiple lists: `fresh (L ++ Xs)`. And the fact `~ In X (L ++ Xs) -> ~ In X L /\ ~ In X Xs` via `in_app_iff` ✓.

OK, let me now think about the `nth` lemmas needed:
- `nth_app_below : forall i l1 l2 d, i < length l1 -> nth i (l1 ++ l2) d = nth i l1 d`.
- `nth_app_at : forall l1 l2 d, nth (length l1) (l1 ++ l2) d = nth 0 l2 d`? Hmm, for the bridge base case i = k = length env: nth k (env ++ [⟦U⟧]) d = ⟦U⟧. That's `nth (length env) (env ++ [x]) d = x`. Prove via `nth_app` or by induction.

Let me recall Stdlib's List lemmas: `nth_app`, `app_nth1`, `app_nth2`? In recent Rocq: `nth_app : forall (l1 l2 : list A) (n : nat), nth n (l1 ++ l2) d = if lt_dec n (length l1) then nth n l1 d else nth (n - length l1) l2 d`. There are lemmas `app_nth1` and `app_nth2` in List. Let's check the installed version's names later.

Alternatively, avoid nth by using a different environment representation: a function `nat -> Rel` where the environment is extended by shifting! E.g., env : nat -> Rel, and "push S" = fun i => match i with 0 => S | S j => env j end. That's simpler for the base cases (nth 0 (push S env) = S; nth (S i) (push S env) = env i) and the bridge can be stated with a shift operation. Let me think:

Define `env` as nat -> Rel. Define `push (S : Rel) (env : nat -> Rel) : nat -> Rel := fun i => match i with 0 => S | S j => env j end`.
Define `pushN (U : ty) (rho : atom -> Rel) (env : nat -> Rel) : nat -> Rel`... hmm, the bridge needs an environment for the free BVars: with lc_ty_at (S k) T, the free BVars are ≤ k. If the environment is a function nat -> Rel, we can define the bridge as:

VR rho (fun i => if i =? k then ⟦U⟧ else env i)? Hmm, the environment for the opened type is env (with BVars < k), and for the unopened T it's env with index k ↦ ⟦U⟧. So:

Bridge: VR rho (fun i => if Nat.eqb i k then ⟦U⟧ else env i) T v <-> VR rho env (open_ty_rec k U T) v.

Hmm, with the "same variable" eqb issue: in the base case i = k, `if k =? k then ...` is stuck but prove `Nat.eqb k k = true` and rewrite ✓ — no problem since there's no requirement that it's definitional. And in the T_TAbs case, the environment pushed at the bound variable: the All clause pushes S via `fun i => match i with 0 => S | S j => env j end`, so the All case's IH would be at k+1 with env' = push S env, and the LHS environment would be `fun i => if i =? (S k) then ⟦U⟧ else push S env i` — hmm, need to show this equals `push S (fun i => if i =? k then ⟦U⟧ else env i)`. For i=0: LHS = push S env 0 = S; RHS = push S (...) 0 = S ✓. For i = S j: LHS = if S j =? S k then ⟦U⟧ else push S env (S j) = if j =? k then ⟦U⟧ else env j; RHS = push S (...) (S j) = (if j =? k then ⟦U⟧ else env j) ✓. So we'd need `Nat.eqb (S j) (S k) = Nat.eqb j k` — true by computation? Nat.eqb is defined by recursion on the first arg: Nat.eqb (S j) (S k) → Nat.eqb j k ✓ definitionally by simpl! Hmm, but the goal `(if S j =? S k then ...)` rewriting — with `simpl` it should reduce to `if j =? k then ...` ✓. And funext needed to prove the function equalities — we have functional extensionality? `funext` is not in Coq core, but Stdlib's FunctionalExtensionality provides it (it's an axiom!). Hmm, "must not use added axioms" — FunctionalExtensionality is an axiom in the standard library. Using it would probably be considered an axiom. Let's avoid it.

To avoid funext, use a list-based environment and the env-agreement lemma with pointwise iff instead of function equality ✓. With a list env, pushing is `S :: env` and the bridge is as before. Base cases use `nth` lemmas ✓, no funext ✓. And where I needed to show two environments agree, use the env-agreement lemma with pointwise iff ✓ (no function equality needed).

Hmm, but the bridge's statement would have `env ++ [⟦U⟧]` and the induction's All case needs `(env ++ [⟦U⟧])` vs `S :: env`: the LHS at the All case: VR rho (env ++ [⟦U⟧]) (Ty_All T2) — the VR All clause pushes S as `S :: (env ++ [⟦U⟧])`. The RHS: VR rho env (Ty_All (open_ty_rec (S k) U T2)) → pushes S as `S :: env`, then interprets `open_ty_rec (S k) U T2` with env' = S :: env. The IH needs, for the unopened side, the environment `(S :: env) ++ [⟦U⟧]`. But the LHS has `S :: (env ++ [⟦U⟧])`. These are equal by list associativity/app lemmas: `(S :: env) ++ [x] = S :: (env ++ [x])` ✓ by `simpl`/`app_comm_cons` — definitional? `(a :: l) ++ m = a :: (l ++ m)` is definitional (cons case of ++) ✓. So `simpl` closes it ✓ or `reflexivity` ✓. Since these are list equalities (no funext), rewriting is fine ✓.

So list-based env is better. ✓

Now, the multi-step lemmas:
```
Lemma multi_trans : forall a b c, a -->* b -> b -->* c -> a -->* c.
Lemma multi_app1 : forall t1 t1' t2, t1 -->* t1' -> locally_closed_tm t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 -->* tm_app t1' t2.
Lemma multi_app2 : forall v1 t2 t2', value v1 -> t2 -->* t2' -> tm_app v1 t2 -->* tm_app v1 t2'.

Lemma multi_choice_left : forall t1 t2, locally_closed_tm t1 -> locally_closed_tm t2 -> tm_choice t1 t2 -->* t1.
Lemma multi_choice_right ...
Lemma multi_app_abs : ... maybe not needed.
```
multi_app1 by induction on the multi derivation ✓ (using ST_App1). multi_app2 similar ✓ (using ST_App2). multi_tapp similar ✓.

- The final application: fundamental at Delta = [], Gamma = empty, sigma = id, gamma = id, rho = fun _ _ => False.
  Conclusion: TR rho [] (Ty_All (Arrow (BVar 0) (BVar 0))) (tm_apply id id t) = TR ... t (by tm_apply_identity).
  Unfold TR: exists w, t -->* w ∧ value w ∧ VR rho [] (All ...) w.

  Get b: tapp w U -->* b, value b, VR rho [S] (Arrow (BVar 0) (BVar 0)) b.
  Unfold the Arrow VR: forall a, value a ∧ VR rho [S] (BVar 0) a -> exists c, app b a -->* c ∧ value c ∧ VR rho [S] (BVar 0) c.
  Take a := v: value v ✓ and VR rho [S] (BVar 0) v = nth 0 [S] (fun _ => False) v = S v = (v = v) ✓.
  Get c: app b v -->* c, value c, VR rho [S] (BVar 0) c = S c = (c = v). So c = v ✓.
  Compose: tapp t U -->* tapp w U (multi_tapp with lc U ✓), tapp w U -->* b, app b v -->* c = v; and app (tapp t U) v -->* app b v via multi_app1 with lc v ✓.
  Conclude app (tapp t U) v -->* v ✓.

Wait: multi_tapp needs t -->* w and locally_closed_ty U = lc_ty_at 0 U ✓.

Hmm, one thing: the fundamental theorem's conclusion type is the *syntactic* T from the judgment; the target's type is literally `Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0))` ✓ same.

- BVar i: Hlc : i < length env1. Simpl. apply Hagree; auto.
- FVar: simpl; reflexivity-ish (tauto).
- Arrow T1 T2: simpl. split; intros H; intros a Ha; destruct (H a Ha) as [b [Hstep [Hv Hrel]]]; exists b; split; auto; split; auto; apply IHT2; auto. Where IHT2 is the IH at T2; we need Hlc for T2: lc_ty_at (length env1) T2 from lc_ty_arrow inversion ✓. And the argument Ha: `value a /\ VR rho env1 T1 a` — for the reverse direction we need to convert VR rho env2 T1 a to VR rho env1 T1 a — wait, the Hall in each direction: for the forward direction (env1 → env2), we have H : forall a, value a /\ VR rho env1 T1 a -> ..., and we need forall a, value a /\ VR rho env2 T1 a -> ... So given Ha2 : value a ∧ VR rho env2 T1 a, we need VR rho env1 T1 a (by IHT1), then H a ✓, then convert the result's VR rho env1 T2 b to env2 by IHT2 ✓. ✓
- All T1: simpl. split; intros H A HA S; destruct (H A HA S) as [b [Hs [Hv Hr]]]; exists b; split; auto; split; auto; apply IHT1 with (env1 := S :: env1) (env2 := S :: env2); auto; simpl; split; auto; intros [|i] Hlt u; simpl; auto; apply Hagree; simpl in Hlt; lia.

Hmm, for the All case, the IH IHT1 is about T1 with arbitrary environments. Let me write the first chunk.

```coq
(* ===== Logical relation infrastructure ===== *)

Definition Rel : Type := tm -> Prop.

Definition emp_rel : Rel := fun _ => False.

Fixpoint ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_apply sigma T1) (ty_apply sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_apply sigma T1)
  end.

Fixpoint tm_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_apply sigma T) (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (ty_apply sigma T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  end.
```

Note: `free_ty` etc.

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Auxiliary definitions for the logical relation proof         *)
(* ============================================================ *)

Definition Rel : Type := tm -> Prop.

Definition emp_rel : Rel := fun _ => False.

Fixpoint ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty := ...
Fixpoint tm_apply ...

Fixpoint free_ty (T : ty) : list atom := ...
Fixpoint free_ty_tm (t : tm) : list atom := ...
Fixpoint free_tm_tm (t : tm) : list atom := ...

Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma max_list_ge : forall l (x : atom), In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y l IH]; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - simpl. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eauto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof.
  intros l Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_ge l (fresh l) Hin).
  unfold fresh in H. lia.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin_app : forall l1 l2, ~ In (fresh (l1 ++ l2)) l1 /\ ~ In (fresh (l1 ++ l2)) l2.
Proof.
  intros l1 l2. split; intro H; apply fresh_notin in H; apply in_app_iff in H; tauto.
Qed.
```

- env1 = [fun w => VR rho' [] (Ty_FVar X) w], env2 = [S].
- Agreement at index 0: forall u, nth 0 env1 d u <-> nth 0 env2 d u, i.e., (fun w => VR rho' [] (Ty_FVar X) w) u <-> S u, i.e., VR rho' [] (Ty_FVar X) u <-> S u, i.e., rho' X u <-> S u. And rho' X = (fun Y => if Y =? X then S else rho Y) X = if X =? X then S else rho X = S (by Nat.eqb_refl) ✓.
So the agreement holds ✓. Then VR rho' [S] T1 b' ✓. Then VR_rho_agree → VR rho [S] T1 b' ✓.

Now `lc_tm_open_inv`:
```
Lemma lc_tm_open_inv : forall K k u t, lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_rec k u t) -> lc_tm_at K (S k) t.
```
Proof by induction on t, generalizing K k:
- tm_bvar i: simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb k i) eqn:E.
  + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst i. (* open = u; premise lc_tm_at K k u *) constructor. lia.
  + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. (* open = bvar i; premise lc_tm_at K k (bvar i) → i < k *) constructor; lia.
- tm_fvar: constructor.
- tm_abs T t1: simpl. intro H. apply lc_tm_abs.
  + inversion H; assumption.
  + apply IHt1 with (k := S k). inversion H; assumption. 
  Hmm, careful with the induction hypothesis and the variable k: `induction t; intros K k u H`. Then for tm_abs, the IH is `forall K k u, lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_rec k u t1) -> lc_tm_at K (S k) t1` ✓. Apply with k := S k ✓ because open_tm_rec k (abs T t1) = abs T (open_tm_rec (S k) u t1). ✓
- tm_app t1 t2: simpl. inversion H; constructor; eapply IHt1; eauto; eapply IHt2; eauto.
- tm_tabs t1: simpl. intro H. apply lc_tm_tabs. inversion H. eapply IHt1; eauto. 
  Hmm: open_tm_rec k u (tabs t1) = tabs (open_tm_rec k u t1); lc_tm_at K k (tabs ...) → lc_tm_at (S K) k (open_tm_rec k u t1); IHt1 with K := S K, k := k gives lc_tm_at (S K) (S k) t1; then lc_tm_tabs with K, k gives lc_tm_at K k (tabs t1) ✓.
- tm_tapp t1 T: similar.
- tm_choice: similar.

`has_type_lc_ty`:
```
Lemma has_type_lc_ty : forall Delta Gamma t T, has_type Delta Gamma t T -> lc_ty_at 0 T.
Proof.
  intros Delta Gamma t T H. induction H.
  - apply wf_ty_lc; assumption.
  - apply lc_ty_arrow; [apply wf_ty_lc; assumption | assumption].
  - assumption.
  - apply lc_ty_all. 
    pose (X := fresh L). assert (HX : ~ In X L) by apply fresh_notin.
    specialize (H0 X HX). (* IH : lc_ty_at 0 (open_ty T (FVar X)) *)
    apply (lc_ty_open_inv 0 (Ty_FVar X) T). assumption.
  - (* T_TApp: IH : lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) ; Hwf : wf_ty Delta U *)

    (* lc_ty_at (S 0) T from lc_ty_at 0 (Ty_All T) : lc_ty_at 1 T ✓ *)

  - assumption.
Qed.
```

Chunk 1 content:

```coq
(* ================================================================ *)
(* Logical relation infrastructure (added for the free theorem)     *)
(* ================================================================ *)

Definition Rel : Type := tm -> Prop.

Definition emp_rel : Rel := fun _ => False.

(* Simultaneous type-variable substitution *)
Fixpoint ty_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sigma X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (ty_apply sigma T1) (ty_apply sigma T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (ty_apply sigma T1)
  end.

(* Simultaneous substitution of type variables and term variables *)
Fixpoint tm_apply (sigma : atom -> ty) (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (ty_apply sigma T) (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (tm_apply sigma gamma t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (ty_apply sigma T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (tm_apply sigma gamma t1) (tm_apply sigma gamma t2)
  end.

(* Free type atoms of a type / a term, and free term atoms of a term *)
Fixpoint free_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => free_ty T1 ++ free_ty T2
  | Ty_All T1 => free_ty T1
  end.

Fixpoint free_ty_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => free_ty T ++ free_ty_tm t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty_tm t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => free_ty_tm t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => free_ty_tm t1 ++ free_ty_tm t2
  end.

Fixpoint free_tm_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs _ t1 => free_tm_tm t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => free_tm_tm t1 ++ free_tm_tm t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => free_tm_tm t1
  | tm_tapp t1 _ => free_tm_tm t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => free_tm_tm t1 ++ free_tm_tm t2
  end.

Fixpoint free_ty_ctx (Gamma : context) : list atom :=
  match Gamma with
  | [] => []
  | (_, T) :: Gamma' => free_ty T ++ free_ty_ctx Gamma'
  end.

Fixpoint max_list (l : list atom) : atom :=
  match l with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: l' => Nat.max x (max_list l')
  end.

Definition fresh (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).

Lemma max_list_ge : forall l (x : atom), In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| y l IH]; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - simpl. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eauto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh l) l.
Proof.
  intros l Hin. pose proof (max_list_ge l (fresh l) Hin). unfold fresh in H. lia.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin_app : forall l1 l2, ~ In (fresh (l1 ++ l2)) l1.
Proof.
  intros l1 l2 H. apply fresh_notin in H. apply in_app_iff in H. tauto.
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin_app2 : forall l1 l2, ~ In (fresh (l1 ++ l2)) l2.
Proof.
  intros l1 l2 H. apply fresh_notin in H. apply in_app_iff in H. tauto.
Qed.
```
